I’ll try to make myself focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry leads to another. Before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, running through every way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different areas of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just one thing. It jumps around a lot — work, like whether this lab partnership could mean I lose my position, then whether my data are wrong, then money and bills, my family thinking I’m behind, and grad school. It feels like my brain just keeps finding a new thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constantly worried like this?\n\nPatient: About seven months now. It started around when the lab’s funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run. Since then it’s just felt pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control the worrying, or does it feel hard to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: It feels really hard to stop once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself, just focus on this one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry triggers another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen and my mind is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. At work especially, I feel really wound up, like I can’t just sit still in my chair. I’ll be tapping my foot, checking my messages over and over, or walking back to the instrument room even when I don’t really need to. And normal lab noises make me jump more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it closely. Some nights are better than others, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a definite problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything over and over, like checking, repeating, or mental rituals, to try to make the worries go away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s like a ritual to make the worry go away. It’s more that I feel unsure and I want to be certain, but I haven’t really noticed a specific repeated thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking the same work or messages even after you already know they’re okay?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I do look back at things sometimes, like messages or work stuff, but I don’t know if it’s repeatedly after I know it’s okay. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been depressed most of the time, but I also haven’t been paying close attention to it. Some days I feel kind of low, but I don’t know if I’d call it depression.\n\nDoctor: If the problems at your lab suddenly got resolved, do you think most of this worrying would still be there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still be there. The lab situation is a big part of it, but my mind just jumps to other things too—like whether my data is wrong, bills piling up, my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. So even if that partnership issue went away, I don’t think the worrying would just stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll try to make myself focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry leads to another. Before I know it I’m just staring at the screen, running through every way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different areas of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just one thing. It jumps around a lot — work, like whether this lab partnership could mean I lose my position, then whether my data are wrong, then money and bills, my family thinking I’m behind, and grad school. It feels like my brain just keeps finding a new thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this constantly worried like this?\n\nPatient: About seven months now. It started around when the lab’s funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run. Since then it’s just felt pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control the worrying, or does it feel hard to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: It feels really hard to stop once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself, just focus on this one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry triggers another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen and my mind is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. At work especially, I feel really wound up, like I can’t just sit still in my chair. I’ll be tapping my foot, checking my messages over and over, or walking back to the instrument room even when I don’t really need to. And normal lab noises make me jump more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it closely. Some nights are better than others, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a definite problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything over and over, like checking, repeating, or mental rituals, to try to make the worries go away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. By the evening I’ll have a headache and feel stiff, almost like I’ve been carrying equipment around all day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the day nearly every day. I’ve had some low moments, but I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once it starts, it’s really hard to shut off. I’ll try to tell myself to just focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry leads to another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen while my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I still do things I usually like, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether I’m enjoying them less. Nothing really stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check the same thing again and again because you feel you have to be completely sure?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that in a way where I feel like I have to keep checking the same thing over and over, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Since then it’s just felt pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control the worrying, or does it feel hard to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: It feels really hard to stop once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself, just focus on this one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry triggers another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen and my mind is going through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. At work especially, I feel really wound up, like I can’t just sit still in my chair. I’ll be tapping my foot, checking my messages over and over, or walking back to the instrument room even when I don’t really need to. And normal lab noises make me jump more than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it closely. Some nights are better than others, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a definite problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything over and over, like checking, repeating, or mental rituals, to try to make the worries go away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s like a ritual to make the worry go away. It’s more that I feel unsure and I want to be certain, but I haven’t really noticed a specific repeated thing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking the same work or messages even after you already know they’re okay?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I do look back at things sometimes, like messages or work stuff, but I don’t know if it’s repeatedly after I know it’s okay. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve been depressed most of the time, but I also haven’t been paying close attention to it. Some days I feel kind of low, but I don’t know if I’d call it depression.\n\nDoctor: If the problems at your lab suddenly got resolved, do you think most of this worrying would still be there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still be there. The lab situation is a big part of it, but my mind just jumps to other things too—like whether my data is wrong, bills piling up, my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already messed up my chances at grad school. So even if that partnership issue went away, I don’t think the worrying would just stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I do zone out sometimes or have to reread things, but I haven’t really tracked whether it’s a concentration problem or just being distracted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. My shoulders and neck are tight a lot during the day. I’ll be working on spreadsheets and realize I’m clenching my jaw. By the evening I’ll have a headache and feel stiff, almost like I’ve been carrying equipment around all day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the day nearly every day. I’ve had some low moments, but I haven’t really been tracking it like that.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once it starts, it’s really hard to shut off. I’ll try to tell myself to just focus on one thing, like finishing a report or checking an assay, but then one worry leads to another. Pretty soon I’m just staring at the screen while my head is running through every possible way things could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I still do things I usually like, but I haven’t been paying close attention to whether I’m enjoying them less. Nothing really stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check the same thing again and again because you feel you have to be completely sure?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that in a way where I feel like I have to keep checking the same thing over and over, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m in bed all night, I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like I already pulled an all-nighter. By the time I’m doing simple things like labeling samples at work or trying to cook dinner for my family, my body feels heavy and my brain just feels drained.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say nearly every day. I’ve had some low moments, but I haven’t really been tracking it, and I don’t know if it’s been that constant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even if I’m in bed all night, I wake up feeling like I barely slept, like I already pulled an all-nighter.
